Yeah, a few things like that. Not like I forget where I am or anything, but I’ll forget something I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I was supposed to call them back, and then the day just sort of went by.

Same with getting out of the house on time for my cousin’s appointments. I’ll think we’ve got plenty of time, and then suddenly we’re rushing.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.

Same with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.

I’ll think we’ve got plenty of time, and then suddenly we’re rushing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself confused about the day, where you are, or who someone is?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what day it is most of the time—well, unless it’s one of those weeks where the trash schedule is different, then I’ll get that mixed up—but I’m not confused about where I am or who people are.\n\nI haven’t had anything like looking at someone and not knowing them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, navigating around your house, or recognizing familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.

The bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.

And then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.

I mean, I know what day it is most of the time—well, unless it’s one of those weeks where the trash schedule is different, then I’ll get that mixed up—but I’m not confused about where I am or who people are.\n\nI haven’t had anything like looking at someone and not knowing them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, navigating around your house, or recognizing familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.

Like if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.

With movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.

I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble following conversations, especially when there are multiple people talking, or keeping up with shows or movies that have complicated plots?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.\n\nWith movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, we’ve got a lot going on with family stuff, and she keeps track of it better than I do, but still… I’ll be sitting there like I heard it, and then it’s just not there. That’s really what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with remembering things your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: It’s been, let me think… noticeably worse for about 11 months, I’d say. Around the time my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That’s when there started being more things to keep track of, and my wife would tell me something and I’d just… not hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory besides having trouble remembering what your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a few things like that. Not like I forget where I am or anything, but I’ll forget something I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I was supposed to call them back, and then the day just sort of went by.\n\nOr I’ll mean to pick up a prescription—there’s a pharmacy we use that’s right near where we get gas, actually, and I’ll even drive past it sometimes—and then later my wife asks if I got it and I realize I didn’t. Same with getting out of the house on time for my cousin’s appointments. I’ll think we’ve got plenty of time, and then suddenly we’re rushing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself confused about the day, where you are, or who someone is?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what day it is most of the time—well, unless it’s one of those weeks where the trash schedule is different, then I’ll get that mixed up—but I’m not confused about where I am or who people are.\n\nI haven’t had anything like looking at someone and not knowing them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, navigating around your house, or recognizing familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble following conversations, especially when there are multiple people talking, or keeping up with shows or movies that have complicated plots?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.\n\nWith movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble doing things you’ve done for years, like cooking a meal, driving somewhere familiar, or handling tools or household gadgets—like you know what to do but your body just doesn’t seem to carry it out right?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I’m driving to the grocery store or the church, I know how to get there, and my hands and feet do what they’re supposed to do.\n\nCooking—well, my wife would say I make a mess, but that’s been true for years. I can use the coffee maker, tools, the remote… although the remote has too many buttons now, honestly. But I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I know what to do and my body won’t do it. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah… some, I guess. Not like I forget the whole day, usually, but I’ll forget things I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I meant to call them back, and then suddenly it’s dinner and I never did it.

Or I’ll be supposed to pick up a prescription, and I’ll drive past the place or come home and remember when I see something on the counter. And leaving the house on time—especially if I’m taking my cousin to an appointment—somehow I think I’ve got plenty of time, then I’m looking for my keys or checking one more thing, and we’re rushing. My cousin hates being late, too, which I understand. I hate waiting rooms, personally, all those old magazines… but, yeah, I’ve been forgetting those kinds of recent things more lately.

Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble following conversations, especially when there are multiple people talking, or keeping up with shows or movies that have complicated plots?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.\n\nWith movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble doing things you’ve done for years, like cooking a meal, driving somewhere familiar, or handling tools or household gadgets—like you know what to do but your body just doesn’t seem to carry it out right?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I’m driving to the grocery store or the church, I know how to get there, and my hands and feet do what they’re supposed to do.\n\nCooking—well, my wife would say I make a mess, but that’s been true for years. I can use the coffee maker, tools, the remote… although the remote has too many buttons now, honestly. But I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I know what to do and my body won’t do it. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with others socially, like saying things that might be inappropriate or not understanding how to act in certain social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. I mean, I’ve probably put my foot in my mouth now and then—my wife could give you a list, I’m sure—but that’s more just me being me, I think.\n\nLike at church coffee hour, sometimes I’ll start telling a story and then realize I’ve gone on too long about something nobody asked about, like parking or the price of deli meat, which somehow always annoys me more than it should. But I don’t think I’m saying inappropriate things or not knowing how to act. I can read the room well enough, I think. At least nobody’s pulled me aside and said I’m acting strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like conversations you had earlier in the day or what you did yesterday?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. Not like I forget the whole day, usually, but I’ll forget things I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I meant to call them back, and then suddenly it’s dinner and I never did it.\n\nOr I’ll be supposed to pick up a prescription, and I’ll drive past the place or come home and remember when I see something on the counter. And leaving the house on time—especially if I’m taking my cousin to an appointment—somehow I think I’ve got plenty of time, then I’m looking for my keys or checking one more thing, and we’re rushing. My cousin hates being late, too, which I understand. I hate waiting rooms, personally, all those old magazines… but, yeah, I’ve been forgetting those kinds of recent things more lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hmm… from the past? I’m not sure. Last week and last month kind of blur together sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s memory or just because one week looks like the next when you’re not working.

Like, if you ask me what we had for dinner last Tuesday, forget it—but I don’t think I ever knew that kind of thing very well. Bigger things, like a family gathering or something at church, I usually remember once somebody gives me a clue. Though sometimes I’ll get hung up trying to place whether something happened before or after some other thing… like was that before the roadwork on Maple, or after? That roadwork took forever.

So… I guess I haven’t noticed losing old memories exactly. It’s more I may need a reminder to put the time and details together.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s my wife noticing it, I guess. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments, or who needs a ride where, that kind of thing—and I’m looking right at her, nodding along. Then a minute later I realize I only caught, I don’t know, half of it. Maybe less.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore her. I mean, we’ve got a lot going on with family stuff, and she keeps track of it better than I do, but still… I’ll be sitting there like I heard it, and then it’s just not there. That’s really what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with remembering things your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: It’s been, let me think… noticeably worse for about 11 months, I’d say. Around the time my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That’s when there started being more things to keep track of, and my wife would tell me something and I’d just… not hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory besides having trouble remembering what your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a few things like that. Not like I forget where I am or anything, but I’ll forget something I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I was supposed to call them back, and then the day just sort of went by.\n\nOr I’ll mean to pick up a prescription—there’s a pharmacy we use that’s right near where we get gas, actually, and I’ll even drive past it sometimes—and then later my wife asks if I got it and I realize I didn’t. Same with getting out of the house on time for my cousin’s appointments. I’ll think we’ve got plenty of time, and then suddenly we’re rushing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself confused about the day, where you are, or who someone is?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what day it is most of the time—well, unless it’s one of those weeks where the trash schedule is different, then I’ll get that mixed up—but I’m not confused about where I am or who people are.\n\nI haven’t had anything like looking at someone and not knowing them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, navigating around your house, or recognizing familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble following conversations, especially when there are multiple people talking, or keeping up with shows or movies that have complicated plots?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.\n\nWith movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble doing things you’ve done for years, like cooking a meal, driving somewhere familiar, or handling tools or household gadgets—like you know what to do but your body just doesn’t seem to carry it out right?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I’m driving to the grocery store or the church, I know how to get there, and my hands and feet do what they’re supposed to do.\n\nCooking—well, my wife would say I make a mess, but that’s been true for years. I can use the coffee maker, tools, the remote… although the remote has too many buttons now, honestly. But I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I know what to do and my body won’t do it. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with others socially, like saying things that might be inappropriate or not understanding how to act in certain social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. I mean, I’ve probably put my foot in my mouth now and then—my wife could give you a list, I’m sure—but that’s more just me being me, I think.\n\nLike at church coffee hour, sometimes I’ll start telling a story and then realize I’ve gone on too long about something nobody asked about, like parking or the price of deli meat, which somehow always annoys me more than it should. But I don’t think I’m saying inappropriate things or not knowing how to act. I can read the room well enough, I think. At least nobody’s pulled me aside and said I’m acting strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like conversations you had earlier in the day or what you did yesterday?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. Not like I forget the whole day, usually, but I’ll forget things I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I meant to call them back, and then suddenly it’s dinner and I never did it.\n\nOr I’ll be supposed to pick up a prescription, and I’ll drive past the place or come home and remember when I see something on the counter. And leaving the house on time—especially if I’m taking my cousin to an appointment—somehow I think I’ve got plenty of time, then I’m looking for my keys or checking one more thing, and we’re rushing. My cousin hates being late, too, which I understand. I hate waiting rooms, personally, all those old magazines… but, yeah, I’ve been forgetting those kinds of recent things more lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to remember things from the past, like events from last week, last month, or even further back?\n\nPatient: Hmm… from the past? I’m not sure. Last week and last month kind of blur together sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s memory or just because one week looks like the next when you’re not working.\n\nLike, if you ask me what we had for dinner last Tuesday, forget it—but I don’t think I ever knew that kind of thing very well. Bigger things, like a family gathering or something at church, I usually remember once somebody gives me a clue. Though sometimes I’ll get hung up trying to place whether something happened before or after some other thing… like was that before the roadwork on Maple, or after? That roadwork took forever.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed losing old memories exactly. It’s more I may need a reminder to put the time and details together.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your memory problems or difficulty keeping track of tasks have gotten noticeably worse over time, to the point where they’re affecting your ability to manage everyday responsibilities like work, family obligations, or personal affairs?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say everything’s falling apart or anything, but it has started to cause some problems. My wife gets frustrated because with my cousin’s care, she feels like she can’t totally count on me to keep track of things. And I get that—if there’s an appointment or something that needs doing, I don’t always stay on top of it the way I should.

And then with the little catering jobs I still help with sometimes, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or go back and redo things so I don’t mix up some small task. You know, like making sure the right tray goes where it’s supposed to—though honestly those labels they use are never as clear as people think they are. But yes, I guess it’s noticeable enough now that it’s causing tension at home and making me have to be more careful with things.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF02.80, F02.81, F02.82, F02.83, F02.84, F02.85\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s my wife noticing it, I guess. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments, or who needs a ride where, that kind of thing—and I’m looking right at her, nodding along. Then a minute later I realize I only caught, I don’t know, half of it. Maybe less.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore her. I mean, we’ve got a lot going on with family stuff, and she keeps track of it better than I do, but still… I’ll be sitting there like I heard it, and then it’s just not there. That’s really what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with remembering things your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: It’s been, let me think… noticeably worse for about 11 months, I’d say. Around the time my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That’s when there started being more things to keep track of, and my wife would tell me something and I’d just… not hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory besides having trouble remembering what your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a few things like that. Not like I forget where I am or anything, but I’ll forget something I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I was supposed to call them back, and then the day just sort of went by.\n\nOr I’ll mean to pick up a prescription—there’s a pharmacy we use that’s right near where we get gas, actually, and I’ll even drive past it sometimes—and then later my wife asks if I got it and I realize I didn’t. Same with getting out of the house on time for my cousin’s appointments. I’ll think we’ve got plenty of time, and then suddenly we’re rushing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself confused about the day, where you are, or who someone is?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what day it is most of the time—well, unless it’s one of those weeks where the trash schedule is different, then I’ll get that mixed up—but I’m not confused about where I am or who people are.\n\nI haven’t had anything like looking at someone and not knowing them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, navigating around your house, or recognizing familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble following conversations, especially when there are multiple people talking, or keeping up with shows or movies that have complicated plots?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.\n\nWith movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble doing things you’ve done for years, like cooking a meal, driving somewhere familiar, or handling tools or household gadgets—like you know what to do but your body just doesn’t seem to carry it out right?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I’m driving to the grocery store or the church, I know how to get there, and my hands and feet do what they’re supposed to do.\n\nCooking—well, my wife would say I make a mess, but that’s been true for years. I can use the coffee maker, tools, the remote… although the remote has too many buttons now, honestly. But I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I know what to do and my body won’t do it. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with others socially, like saying things that might be inappropriate or not understanding how to act in certain social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. I mean, I’ve probably put my foot in my mouth now and then—my wife could give you a list, I’m sure—but that’s more just me being me, I think.\n\nLike at church coffee hour, sometimes I’ll start telling a story and then realize I’ve gone on too long about something nobody asked about, like parking or the price of deli meat, which somehow always annoys me more than it should. But I don’t think I’m saying inappropriate things or not knowing how to act. I can read the room well enough, I think. At least nobody’s pulled me aside and said I’m acting strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like conversations you had earlier in the day or what you did yesterday?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. I mean, if I’m driving to the grocery store or the church, I know how to get there, and my hands and feet do what they’re supposed to do.\n\nCooking—well, my wife would say I make a mess, but that’s been true for years. I can use the coffee maker, tools, the remote… although the remote has too many buttons now, honestly. But I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I know what to do and my body won’t do it. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with others socially, like saying things that might be inappropriate or not understanding how to act in certain social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. I mean, I’ve probably put my foot in my mouth now and then—my wife could give you a list, I’m sure—but that’s more just me being me, I think.\n\nLike at church coffee hour, sometimes I’ll start telling a story and then realize I’ve gone on too long about something nobody asked about, like parking or the price of deli meat, which somehow always annoys me more than it should. But I don’t think I’m saying inappropriate things or not knowing how to act. I can read the room well enough, I think. At least nobody’s pulled me aside and said I’m acting strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like conversations you had earlier in the day or what you did yesterday?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. Not like I forget the whole day, usually, but I’ll forget things I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I meant to call them back, and then suddenly it’s dinner and I never did it.\n\nOr I’ll be supposed to pick up a prescription, and I’ll drive past the place or come home and remember when I see something on the counter. And leaving the house on time—especially if I’m taking my cousin to an appointment—somehow I think I’ve got plenty of time, then I’m looking for my keys or checking one more thing, and we’re rushing. My cousin hates being late, too, which I understand. I hate waiting rooms, personally, all those old magazines… but, yeah, I’ve been forgetting those kinds of recent things more lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to remember things from the past, like events from last week, last month, or even further back?\n\nPatient: Hmm… from the past? I’m not sure. Last week and last month kind of blur together sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s memory or just because one week looks like the next when you’re not working.\n\nLike, if you ask me what we had for dinner last Tuesday, forget it—but I don’t think I ever knew that kind of thing very well. Bigger things, like a family gathering or something at church, I usually remember once somebody gives me a clue. Though sometimes I’ll get hung up trying to place whether something happened before or after some other thing… like was that before the roadwork on Maple, or after? That roadwork took forever.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed losing old memories exactly. It’s more I may need a reminder to put the time and details together.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your memory problems or difficulty keeping track of tasks have gotten noticeably worse over time, to the point where they’re affecting your ability to manage everyday responsibilities like work, family obligations, or personal affairs?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say everything’s falling apart or anything, but it has started to cause some problems. My wife gets frustrated because with my cousin’s care, she feels like she can’t totally count on me to keep track of things. And I get that—if there’s an appointment or something that needs doing, I don’t always stay on top of it the way I should.\n\nAnd then with the little catering jobs I still help with sometimes, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or go back and redo things so I don’t mix up some small task. You know, like making sure the right tray goes where it’s supposed to—though honestly those labels they use are never as clear as people think they are. But yes, I guess it’s noticeable enough now that it’s causing tension at home and making me have to be more careful with things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s my wife noticing it, I guess. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments, or who needs a ride where, that kind of thing—and I’m looking right at her, nodding along. Then a minute later I realize I only caught, I don’t know, half of it. Maybe less.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore her. I mean, we’ve got a lot going on with family stuff, and she keeps track of it better than I do, but still… I’ll be sitting there like I heard it, and then it’s just not there. That’s really what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with remembering things your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: It’s been, let me think… noticeably worse for about 11 months, I’d say. Around the time my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. That’s when there started being more things to keep track of, and my wife would tell me something and I’d just… not hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory besides having trouble remembering what your wife tells you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a few things like that. Not like I forget where I am or anything, but I’ll forget something I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I was supposed to call them back, and then the day just sort of went by.\n\nOr I’ll mean to pick up a prescription—there’s a pharmacy we use that’s right near where we get gas, actually, and I’ll even drive past it sometimes—and then later my wife asks if I got it and I realize I didn’t. Same with getting out of the house on time for my cousin’s appointments. I’ll think we’ve got plenty of time, and then suddenly we’re rushing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself confused about the day, where you are, or who someone is?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what day it is most of the time—well, unless it’s one of those weeks where the trash schedule is different, then I’ll get that mixed up—but I’m not confused about where I am or who people are.\n\nI haven’t had anything like looking at someone and not knowing them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like judging distances, navigating around your house, or recognizing familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I get around the house fine, and places I know look familiar to me. Distances… I mean, I’m not great at backing the car into tight spots, but I don’t think that’s new. My wife would probably say I’ve always been like that. So no, nothing I’ve really noticed that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble figuring out which direction you're facing, or following directions to get somewhere new?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m going someplace new, I’ll usually use the phone directions, and sometimes I’ll miss a turn because I’m thinking about something else, or the voice says “in 500 feet” and I’m like, well, how far is that really—my wife always says I wait too long to get over.\n\nBut I don’t think I get mixed up about what direction I’m facing, exactly. I can follow directions if I’m paying attention. I haven’t noticed anything like getting lost somewhere I should be able to figure out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to plan or organize things, like managing your schedule or keeping track of tasks that need to get done?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say I can’t plan anything, but lately I’ll have something I’m supposed to do and it just kind of slips off the table. Like I meant to return a call from the doctor’s office, and then I remembered it later when they were already closed.\n\nSame with picking up a prescription—I’ll think, okay, I’ll do that after lunch, and then I end up doing three other little things, or I start looking for something in the garage, and next thing I know it’s dinner. And I’ve been late leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments, which is embarrassing because I’m trying to help, not make things harder. So… yeah, keeping track of tasks has been a bit harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with tasks like reading a map, judging distances while driving, or noticing objects in your peripheral vision?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… maps, I don’t really use paper maps much anymore, honestly. I use the phone like everybody does, though my brother still keeps an atlas in his truck, which I always thought was kind of funny.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything like bumping into things or not seeing cars next to me or anything. Driving distance seems about the same as it’s always been. I mean, I’m cautious with changing lanes, but that’s not new. So I don’t think there’s been a problem there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing everyday objects, like not knowing what a spoon or a phone is used for, or being unsure how to use common items around the house?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what things are for around the house—spoons, phone, remote, all that.\n\nSometimes I’ll put something down and then wonder where I left it, but that’s not the same as not knowing what it is. My wife has this one kitchen gadget, though, for slicing… I don’t know, zucchini or something, and I always think it looks like it belongs in a toolbox. But common stuff, no, I haven’t had trouble recognizing it or using it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble finding the right words when you're trying to say something, or understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much finding the words. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause, but everybody does that.\n\nThe bigger thing is understanding—or maybe not understanding, more like not absorbing it. What really got me to come in is my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride and when, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, and then a minute later I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she said.\n\nAnd then I have to ask her again, which, you know, she gets frustrated, and I don’t blame her. It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. It’s just… it doesn’t stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble following conversations, especially when there are multiple people talking, or keeping up with shows or movies that have complicated plots?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if there’s a lot going on, I can lose the thread. Not that I don’t understand the words, exactly. It’s more like I start out following fine and then my mind just sort of wanders off.\n\nLike if I’m sitting down to work out a catering order—my wife’s cousin does these church events sometimes, and there’s always way too many trays of sandwiches, in my opinion—or if I’m on one of those parish planning calls, I’ll be listening at first. Then after a while I realize I missed a chunk of it, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never read them.\n\nWith movies, I can usually manage if I’m interested, but if it gets too tangled, I might be asking, “Wait, who’s that again?” So yes, a little trouble keeping up, mostly when I have to stay with it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble doing things you’ve done for years, like cooking a meal, driving somewhere familiar, or handling tools or household gadgets—like you know what to do but your body just doesn’t seem to carry it out right?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I’m driving to the grocery store or the church, I know how to get there, and my hands and feet do what they’re supposed to do.\n\nCooking—well, my wife would say I make a mess, but that’s been true for years. I can use the coffee maker, tools, the remote… although the remote has too many buttons now, honestly. But I don’t think I’ve had that thing where I know what to do and my body won’t do it. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with others socially, like saying things that might be inappropriate or not understanding how to act in certain social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. I mean, I’ve probably put my foot in my mouth now and then—my wife could give you a list, I’m sure—but that’s more just me being me, I think.\n\nLike at church coffee hour, sometimes I’ll start telling a story and then realize I’ve gone on too long about something nobody asked about, like parking or the price of deli meat, which somehow always annoys me more than it should. But I don’t think I’m saying inappropriate things or not knowing how to act. I can read the room well enough, I think. At least nobody’s pulled me aside and said I’m acting strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like conversations you had earlier in the day or what you did yesterday?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. Not like I forget the whole day, usually, but I’ll forget things I meant to do. Like the doctor’s office called and I meant to call them back, and then suddenly it’s dinner and I never did it.\n\nOr I’ll be supposed to pick up a prescription, and I’ll drive past the place or come home and remember when I see something on the counter. And leaving the house on time—especially if I’m taking my cousin to an appointment—somehow I think I’ve got plenty of time, then I’m looking for my keys or checking one more thing, and we’re rushing. My cousin hates being late, too, which I understand. I hate waiting rooms, personally, all those old magazines… but, yeah, I’ve been forgetting those kinds of recent things more lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to remember things from the past, like events from last week, last month, or even further back?\n\nPatient: Hmm… from the past? I’m not sure. Last week and last month kind of blur together sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s memory or just because one week looks like the next when you’re not working.\n\nLike, if you ask me what we had for dinner last Tuesday, forget it—but I don’t think I ever knew that kind of thing very well. Bigger things, like a family gathering or something at church, I usually remember once somebody gives me a clue. Though sometimes I’ll get hung up trying to place whether something happened before or after some other thing… like was that before the roadwork on Maple, or after? That roadwork took forever.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed losing old memories exactly. It’s more I may need a reminder to put the time and details together.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your memory problems or difficulty keeping track of tasks have gotten noticeably worse over time, to the point where they’re affecting your ability to manage everyday responsibilities like work, family obligations, or personal affairs?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say everything’s falling apart or anything, but it has started to cause some problems. My wife gets frustrated because with my cousin’s care, she feels like she can’t totally count on me to keep track of things. And I get that—if there’s an appointment or something that needs doing, I don’t always stay on top of it the way I should.\n\nAnd then with the little catering jobs I still help with sometimes, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or go back and redo things so I don’t mix up some small task. You know, like making sure the right tray goes where it’s supposed to—though honestly those labels they use are never as clear as people think they are. But yes, I guess it’s noticeable enough now that it’s causing tension at home and making me have to be more careful with things.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing familiar faces or objects, or figuring out what something is just by looking at it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, faces… I still know people I know. Sometimes I’ll blank on a name, but I recognize them. Objects too—I don’t think I’ve ever looked at something familiar and not known what it was.\n\nUnless you mean like when there’s some weird kitchen gadget in a drawer and I’m thinking, “What is this even for?” But that’s probably because my wife buys these things and they all look like they came from one of those TV ads. So… no, not really trouble recognizing things by looking at them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing common objects, like a spoon or a key, when you're holding them or seeing them, even though your vision or touch isn’t the problem?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. If I’m holding a key, I know it’s a key, or a spoon, same thing. I don’t have to feel around and guess what it is.\n\nNow, I might misplace the keys, which is a whole other story—my wife says I have “special places” that aren’t special at all, they’re just random. But recognizing them? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
